=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array nums consisting of positive integers.
A special subsequence is defined as a subsequence of length 4, represented by indices (p, q, r, s), where p < q < r < s. This subsequence must satisfy the following conditions:

nums[p] * nums[r] == nums[q] * nums[s]
There must be at least one element between each pair of indices. In other words, q - p > 1, r - q > 1 and s - r > 1.

Return the number of different special subsequences in nums.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,4,3,6,1]
Output: 1
Explanation:
There is one special subsequence in nums.

(p, q, r, s) = (0, 2, 4, 6):

	
This corresponds to elements (1, 3, 3, 1).
nums[p] * nums[r] = nums[0] * nums[4] = 1 * 3 = 3
nums[q] * nums[s] = nums[2] * nums[6] = 3 * 1 = 3




Example 2:

Input: nums = [3,4,3,4,3,4,3,4]
Output: 3
Explanation:
There are three special subsequences in nums.

(p, q, r, s) = (0, 2, 4, 6):

	
This corresponds to elements (3, 3, 3, 3).
nums[p] * nums[r] = nums[0] * nums[4] = 3 * 3 = 9
nums[q] * nums[s] = nums[2] * nums[6] = 3 * 3 = 9


(p, q, r, s) = (1, 3, 5, 7):
	
This corresponds to elements (4, 4, 4, 4).
nums[p] * nums[r] = nums[1] * nums[5] = 4 * 4 = 16
nums[q] * nums[s] = nums[3] * nums[7] = 4 * 4 = 16


(p, q, r, s) = (0, 2, 5, 7):
	
This corresponds to elements (3, 3, 4, 4).
nums[p] * nums[r] = nums[0] * nums[5] = 3 * 4 = 12
nums[q] * nums[s] = nums[2] * nums[7] = 3 * 4 = 12




 
Constraints:

7 <= nums.length <= 1000
1 <= nums[i] <= 1000

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

from typing import List
from math import gcd
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        cnt = defaultdict(int)
        ans = 0
        for r in range(2, n - 2):
            q = r - 2
            # add all valid p for this q
            for p in range(q - 1):
                g = gcd(nums[p], nums[q])
                cnt[(nums[p] // g, nums[q] // g)] += 1
            # count with all valid s
            for s in range(r + 2, n):
                g = gcd(nums[s], nums[r])
                ans += cnt.get((nums[s] // g, nums[r] // g), 0)
        return ans

Let's write final code:

```python
from typing import List
from math import gcd
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def numberOfSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        pair_ratio_count = defaultdict(int)
        ans = 0

        for r in range(2, n - 2):
            q = r - 2
            # Add all valid (p, q) pairs ending at q = r - 2.
            for p in range(q - 1):
                g = gcd(nums[p], nums[q])
                pair_ratio_count[(nums[p] // g, nums[q] // g)] += 1

            # Use current r as the third element; s must be at least r+2.
            for s in range(r + 2, n):
                g = gcd(nums[s], nums[r])
                ans += pair_ratio_count.get((nums[s] // g, nums[r] // g), 0)

        return ans
```
